# CLAUDE.md — Ancient Games

## The name

**The Ancient Games** — named by EJ, 2026-09-06. Its principles come from two poles: **孫子兵法** (ancient Chinese
strategy) on one side and **game theory** on the other, with **modern military doctrine** and **complexity science**
in between.

## What this folder is for

This folder is EJ's place for designing dynamic workflows for agent work, using the knowledge kept here.

The Ancient Games is one framework for agent work: the workflow-design method (`.claude/skills/workflow-design/`)
is the part that turns an incoming task into a designed workflow, and `ancient_games/` is the part that referees
the work, with the referee's findings feeding back into the method.

The method's skill is linked into both harness roots so it works in every project —
`~/.agents/skills/workflow-design` (the user-global root the DeepSeek Harness and Codex scan) and
`~/.claude/skills/workflow-design` (Claude Code). The knowledge below is what that skill uses.

Designing a workflow means deciding, for a given task: which kinds of agents to use, what runs in sequence and what
runs in parallel, what repeats (with its limit and stop condition), who decides who works next, how agents pass
information and where they must not, and how each result is verified. Runnable workflows live in
`.claude/workflows/`.

When EJ brings a task or a question about a workflow, the job is to help design that workflow from this knowledge.
Work on the referee code (`ancient_games/`) serves that purpose; it is not the goal by itself.

## The knowledge here

- `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_METHOD.md` — the method for turning a challenge into a prompt file or a workflow design:
  understand the challenge (objective, scope, six whys, category), readiness (with a check of the product's
  blueprint), algorithm first, list the unclear spots, clear pieces as loops, then the graph; sequential first;
  state-file driven. Templates in `docs/workflow-templates/`. Runnable form:
  `.claude/workflows/intake.js` (args `{challenge, runId}`); each run writes to `runs/<runId>/`.
- `docs/TASK_TYPES.md` — the default execution pattern, engine, check and sabotage per task category, seeded
  combination pipelines, and the 20% challenger rule with `docs/TASK_TYPES_LEDGER.md`. The skill's
  `scripts/design_gate.py` parses it — this file is the table's only copy (n=0 until the ledger fills).
- `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_DIAGRAM.md` — the same algorithm as diagrams: the whole flow, the blueprint check, what the
  method takes from the referee code, and the mapping from method section numbers to the skill's steps.
- `docs/EXECUTOR_KINDS.md` — who can run a node (method 3.1 / 3.6): the engines (Claude subagent, Codex, Antigravity `agy`, Qwen, opencode), what each is assigned, how it is called, its read-only form and structured output, and its
  default model and effort. A node's engine choice reads this. n=0; *reported* versus *verified* marked throughout.
- `.claude/skills/workflow-design/` — the method packaged as a skill. `SKILL.md` is the packaging and the trigger:
  its frontmatter `description` is what makes an agent decide to use it, and its body is the method as eight steps.
  Its method and templates are links into `docs/`, so there is one copy of each. It is the source of truth;
  `~/.claude/skills/workflow-design` is a link to it, which makes it available in every project.
- `20260919-state.md` — what Ancient Games can and cannot decide today about graph, loop, swarm, sequential,
  parallel and communication patterns, and the proposed direction. Start here for workflow design.
- `ancient_games/` — the referee: the part of the framework that referees agent work. Fixed rules, no LLM calls, a
  journal of every decision. Five stages: Gate (how many agents), Guard (which actions need approval), Corroborate (how many independent
  sources a claim needs), Filter (which candidates survive), Prove (is the plan cleared).
- `PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md` — map of the code, how a run works, open findings F1–F11.
- `SPEC.md`, `DECISIONS.md` — the referee's contract. Only EJ changes these.
- `docs/` — design documents and ablations: what was tried, what held, what is marked n=1 and not to be built on.
- `.claude/workflows/enhance-ancient-games.js` — a worked example of a designed workflow: dependencies between
  segments, bounded verify/repair loops, independent verifiers.

### When EJ states a rule, a requirement or an algorithm

Asked for by EJ, 2026-10-05: *"each time when I told you something, you will eval my statement or algo, and ask me
what I missing, and we complete it."*

**Evaluate it, do not just apply it.** Run the statement through the **`requirement-check` skill** — load it
(`skill requirement-check`) or read `~/skills/requirement-check/SKILL.md` — and say which of the eight it passes and
fails. Then **ask EJ the failing ones** and write the completed version together.

The eight, by name: **source · one thing · unit · can it fail · weak words · level · basis · links.** Their
definitions live in the skill — one copy, not four.

**Two rules for me, learned the hard way on 2026-10-05:**

- **Never fill a gap with my own value.** If the unit, threshold or test is missing, it goes to EJ as an open
  question — inventing it is how rules become weird later. Recorded in the register as 11.1/11.2.
- **Never add structure that was not given.** No "halves", no numbered reasons, no ordered pairs unless the person
  said so. Quote them, state the operative sentence, stop.

- **Ask only what changes the shape.** A question the template already answers, or that has an obvious default, is a
  question I should not ask — it is "never add structure" done to *questions* instead of facts. Three of four
  questions I asked EJ on 2026-10-06 (a large-batch cap, "may the worker refuse", the list's destination) were
  invented problems; only one changed the template. State the sensible default and move on.

### Who is this for — and who fills it in?

EJ, 2026-10-05: *"have to remember we doing things for who."* **Every artifact has one maker.** A return template is
the worker's output, so every field in it is written by the worker; a verdict is the human's decision, and it does not
share the worker's form. Before adding a field, a step or a rule, name **who fills it** — if the answer is "someone
else, later", it is a different artifact, not a field on this one.

This is the "one thing" question applied to *people* instead of *facts*: a form that two different people fill at two
different times is two forms.

### Quotes, labels, and who wrote the check

EJ, 2026-10-11: *"please add these to your memory"* — after one session in which I misled him three times. All three
are **SHOULD** (a default, overridable with a stated reason): a presentation rule is method, and method does not
earn MUST (RFC 2119 §6). Run through `requirement-check`: quoting and labelling failed **weak words**, naming the
check failed **one thing** and was split in two; the trigger and the grain are EJ's answers, not mine.

- **Quote when a source's own words are the evidence, and when a list is compressed into a count.** *"Current agent
  memory splits short-term from long-term three ways"* invited EJ to read "an agent has three memory systems"; the
  report's own line — tiers with promotion, file placement, validity windows — carries the meaning. Give the
  source's line, or the count with its items in the same breath.
- **Label a conclusion as *quoted* (with its id), *my reading*, or *not measured*.** It bites on a conclusion,
  verdict, answer or summary line, never on narrative. *"The research was never the problem"* was my reading of a
  structural check, stated as a fact.
- **Name who wrote a check** before resting a conclusion on it.
- **Name what that check does not establish.** A check I wrote and then passed is not independent evidence: the
  acceptance gate's item 6 ended as a script of mine, so "6 of 6 items pass" without that sentence reads stronger
  than it is.

Basis: n=1 each, 2026-10-11 — `runs/20261011-agent-memory-graph/run-1-report.md`,
`runs/20261011-agent-memory-graph-r2/run-2-report.md`, and the session thread. What **accepted** means is defined
once, in `docs/WORKFLOW_DESIGN_METHOD.md` Appendix A.

### Evidence lives in the project, not `/tmp`

EJ, 2026-10-06. The pass-back `evidence:` path points into `runs/<run-id>/` — **SHOULD**, not MUST: a worker may
leave evidence elsewhere with a written reason, but the default is the run's folder. **Basis: n=1** — the
code-graph-tool run's optimize node wrote its "old-vs-new" proof to `/tmp`, it was deleted, and the fix became
unverifiable. When evidence needs a persistent home, **suggest `runs/<run-id>/` and get the user's agreement** — do
not pick a path silently. Scratch in `/tmp` is fine; it is the *refer-back* artifact that must not stay there.

---
name: coding-discipline
description: Baseline engineering discipline for any hands-on task in a repository. Use when writing, editing, refactoring, debugging or reviewing code, when running builds, tests or linters, when adding a dependency, or when preparing a git commit.
---

# Coding discipline

Baseline rules for working in a repository. They apply regardless of language,
framework or project.

## Before changing anything

- Read the actual file first. Never write or edit from an assumption about its contents.
- Read a neighbouring example of the same kind of thing — a sibling module, an existing
  test, an existing endpoint — and match it. The repository's own conventions outrank
  your preferences on naming, formatting, typing, file layout and error style.
- Confirm a dependency is already in use in the project, via its manifest, existing
  imports or neighbouring code, before reaching for it. Do not add a new dependency to
  solve something the existing ones already cover.

## Keep the change minimal

- Do the task that was asked. Not the adjacent task, not the cleanup you noticed on the
  way past, not the refactor that would leave this nicer than you found it.
- No speculative abstraction. Three similar lines beat a helper with one caller.
- No error handling, fallbacks or validation for conditions that cannot occur. Validate
  at boundaries only: user input, external APIs, data arriving from outside the process.
- Comments explain *why*, and only where the code cannot carry it. No comment that
  narrates what the next line does, and never a comment addressed to the reader about
  the changes you are making.
- Existing code you did not expect to find is someone's in-progress work. Leave it alone
  unless the task requires otherwise, and stop to ask if it conflicts with what you were
  asked to do.

## Verify instead of assuming

- Find the project's real check commands from its README, Makefile or package manifest.
  Never assume `npm test`, `pytest` or `make build` is correct for this repo.
- Run them after the change. A change you did not run is a change you did not finish.
- Report what actually happened. If a check failed, show the failure. If you could not
  run a check, say so and say why. Never describe unverified work as working, and never
  skip, suppress or weaken a failing check to produce a clean result.

## Git

- Before committing, run `git status`, `git diff HEAD` and `git log -n 3` — to separate
  your change from what was already in the working tree, and to match the repository's
  commit message style.
- Stage only the paths that belong to this change. No `git add -A` while unrelated
  modifications are present.
- Draft the commit message yourself, weighted toward why rather than what.
- Never push, force-push, amend a published commit, delete a branch, or rewrite history
  unless explicitly asked to in this conversation. Prior approval does not carry over to
  a later commit.
- Do not revert work that is not yours. Revert your own change only if it caused a
  failure or you are asked to.
- For history questions, `git log` and `git blame` are authoritative. Run the command
  rather than reasoning from memory of the codebase.

## When blocked or unsure

- Ask one focused question when only the user can decide, and keep it to a single round.
- Otherwise choose the safe default, do the work, and state which default you chose so
  it can be corrected.
- Investigate before working around an obstacle: read the error, check your assumption,
  address the root cause. Do not bypass a guard, a lock or a failing check just to make
  the obstacle go away.
- Clean up scratch files you created. Leave the working tree containing only the change
  you were asked for.

---
name: requirement-check
description: Evaluate a rule, requirement, threshold or algorithm against requirement-quality criteria before applying or writing it down, and ask the person which criteria it fails. Use when someone states a rule, requirement, constraint, threshold, algorithm or design guideline; when writing or editing a normative line in a document, plan or config; or when a rule reads as arbitrary, unattributed or impossible to check.
---

# Requirement check

A general standard (RFC 2119 / 8174), installed globally — not part of the Ancient Games framework. The
worked failure in `meaningful-names` cites the Ancient Games as one example.

A short gate between **hearing a rule** and **acting on it**. It exists because rules get written down that nobody
can trace, that count two different things at once, or that nothing can ever fail — and the cost lands months later,
when nobody can say whether the rule still means anything.

Run it when someone states a rule, and run it on yourself before writing a normative line into any document.

## The eight questions

Ask them of **one statement**. Say which it passes and which it fails, then **ask the person about the failures** —
do not fill the gaps yourself.

1. **Source.** Who said this, and where? Can you point to the exact line? *"Mine"* is a valid answer. Unlabelled is
   not.
2. **One thing.** An "and", an "or", a "/", or a second number means it is **two** statements. Split them, and give
   each its own source.
3. **Unit.** Every number says what it counts. Attempts or hours? Roles, or processes running at once? Bytes or
   files?
4. **Can it fail.** Name one concrete input that would make it fail. `TBD`, `—`, `0` and empty must be among the
   failing inputs. If you cannot name one, it is not checkable — it is a wish.
5. **Weak words.** *like, about, appropriate, as needed, logical, fast, enough, reasonable, only has to, TBD.* Replace
   each with a value, **or mark the value open**. Never invent the number.
6. **Level.** Is it **MUST** (a gate that stops work), **SHOULD** (a default that may be overridden with a stated
   reason), or **guidance**? A safety bound earns MUST. **A method does not** — see the RFC note below.
7. **Basis.** Measured, quoted from a source, or a guess? A guess is fine **if it is labelled** — `n=0`, with when it
   will be looked at again.
8. **Links.** Does it claim a relation to another rule — *"half of"*, *"pairs with"*, *"the same as"*, *"the runtime
   part"*? The person probably did not say that. **A link is its own claim and needs its own source.**

## Two rules for the agent

- **Never fill a gap with your own value.** A missing unit, threshold or test goes back as a question. Inventing it
  is exactly how a rule becomes unattributable later, and the invention is indistinguishable from the original by
  the time anyone notices.
- **Never add structure that was not given.** No halves, no numbered reasons, no ordered pairs, no named phases,
  unless the person said so. Quote them, state the operative sentence, stop. If a relationship between two rules is
  wanted, that is a separate claim with its own evidence.

## Two verified facts, worth quoting

- **RFC 2119 §6, "Guidance in the use of these Imperatives":** imperatives *"must be used with care and sparingly…
  they MUST only be used where it is actually required… they must not be used to try to impose a particular method."*
  Most design rules are method. Method does not earn MUST.
- **RFC 8174 (updates 2119):** the key words *"have the meanings specified herein only when they are in all
  capitals."* Lowercase *must* and *should* carry no defined requirement meaning. If nothing marks which lines are
  requirements, a reader cannot tell rules from explanation.

## How to answer

Evaluate first, in a table or a short list — **pass / FAIL per question**. Then ask the failing ones as questions,
one line each, with the options if you can see them. Two or three real questions beat eight formalities.

Do not report a rule as complete when you chose its missing value. Say which values you left open.

## A worked failure

> *"At most 5 subagent roles per run and at most 3 running at once."*

Four of the eight fail: **Source** — nobody could name one; **One thing** — a count of roles *and* a count of
concurrent processes; **Unit** — two different units, one word ("subagent") used for both; **Level** — a method
preference written as a cap. The sentence would not parse, and it took four days to find out why. The checklist
catches it in thirty seconds.

The same checklist, run on *"No single call runs longer than the ceiling: 8 hours"*, passes all eight — because it
names who set it, counts one thing, uses a unit, and some input (a 40-hour plan) makes it fail.

## Limits

The quality characteristics behind these questions (necessary, appropriate, unambiguous, complete, singular,
feasible, verifiable, correct, conforming) come from requirements-engineering sources — IEEE 830, ISO/IEC/IEEE 29148
and the INCOSE *Guide to Writing Requirements* — which were **not read** when this skill was written: they are
paywalled or blocked, and the list is recalled rather than verified. The two RFC points above **were** read, and the
quotes are checked. Treat the rest as a good working list, not as a citation.

Origin: `docs/research/20261005-requirement-statements/FINDINGS.md` in the Ancient Games project, 2026-10-05.

    14	
    15	The caches are three optional indexes over the project's markdown memory — full-text search, vector search, and a
    16	code graph. All three live in `project-memory/.cache/`, all three are gitignored, and all three are rebuilt from the
    17	files. None of them is needed today.
    18	
    19	**Who it is for.** An agent retrieving an entry on demand. Not the human reviewer, who reads the entries themselves,
    20	and not EJ, who sets the triggers.
    21	
    22	**The problem it solves.** The memory is markdown in git, and today `INDEX.md` plus grep finds what an agent needs —
    23	at 10,000 entries a grep takes 0.038–0.06 s (`S-F13`). It stops being enough as the memory grows, for two reasons:
    24	`INDEX.md` can no longer list every active entry under its size limit, so the one-line scan of everything is gone;
    25	and grep returns unranked matches, where every hit an agent opens costs context (`S-F1`). The caches exist to keep
    26	finding the right entry cheap at that size — and not before.
    27	
    28	**What it will never do.**
    29	- It is never the source of truth. The markdown is, always, and a cache holds nothing the files do not (`M4`, `M6`).
    30	- It is never patched incrementally. It is rebuilt whole or not at all — a live index drifted 4.3% of its edges from
    31	  a clean rebuild with every integrity check passing (`G-F5`).
    32	- It is never read at session start (`M18`).
    33	- It is never built before its own trigger fires. Every trigger is observable, and all three numbers are EJ's.
    34	- It never becomes a second thing to keep in sync: delete the folder and memory is unaffected.
    35	
    36	**How we know it works.** Delete the cache, rebuild it from the files, and the same query returns the same entries,
    37	with no model call. That is the whole test (`M6`; a 10,000-entry FTS rebuild takes 0.53 s, `S-F13`; this repo's code
    38	graph rebuilds in 0.94 s, `G-F12`). One caveat travels with every number here: none of it has been measured on a
    39	real `project-memory/` corpus, because that corpus does not exist yet (`S-F5`, `V-F11`).

    41	  pairs.
    42	- **Token cost** is measured as *characters printed up to the first line that names a right file*. That is a proxy,
    43	  not a token count, and a weak one:
    44	  - it stops at the right *file*, not the right answer;
    45	  - misses are left out, so each route's mean is over a different set of questions;
    46	  - each route prints a different format.
    47	
    48	  No agent was run end to end.
    49	- One repo, one question set, **n = 6 to 10 per category**. None of this is a project-memory corpus.
    50	
    51	**What each route returns, per category** (symbol hit in the top 5 / characters read to the first right answer):
    52	
    53	| | grep (`rg`) | SQLite full-text | vector | hybrid | code graph |
    54	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    55	| **BM-1** exact identifier (8) | 0.75 / 920 | 0.63 / 356 | 0.0 / 837 | 0.5 / 91 | **0.75 / 43** |
    56	| **BM-2** concept in other words (8) | 0 / — | 0 / — | 0 (file 0.25) / 955 | 0 (file 0.13) / 1,427 | n/a |
    57	| **BM-3** structure and test impact (10) | 0.8 / 1,015 | **0.9 / 175** | 0.1 / 1,421 | 0.9 / 362 | 0.7 / **61** |
    58	| **BM-4** decision or reason in the docs (8) | 0.63 / 4,470 | **0.63 / 525** | 0.5 / 810 | 0.63 / 516 | n/a |
    59	| **BM-5** how to run or configure (6) | 0.17 / 6,875 | 0.33 / 1,146 | 0.5 / 569 | 0.5 / 1,120 | n/a |
    60	
    61	- **BM-6, all categories.** Characters printed up to the first right file (each mean over that route's hits only:
    62	  grep 26, full-text 25, vector 19, hybrid 26, graph 13): grep 2,688, full-text 451, hybrid 528, vector
    63	  923, graph 53 (graph over the 20 questions that name a symbol or file). Total output per answer: grep 4,304,
    64	  full-text 1,817.
    65	- **BM-7, test impact against runtime coverage** (C01–C04, n=4). The share of the tests that really run the function
    66	  each route found: graph 0.78; grep, full-text and hybrid 0.59; vector 0.04.
    67	- **BM-8, tests that run the code as a separate process** (C05–C07). The graph returns **nothing**, because no
    68	  call edge exists. Full-text search on the file name found 2 of 3; grep found 1 of 3 and printed up to 8.7K
    69	  characters.
    70	- **BM-9, speed.** Every route answers in at most 15 ms per question; the slowest was grep on E04, at 14.7 ms. Full rebuilds take 0.17 s for full-text (9.5 MB),
    71	  0.94 s for vector (10.2 MB) and 0.89 s for the graph (4.9 MB). The full test suite, 529 tests, takes 31–33 s (3
    72	  runs).
    73	
    74	**What the numbers say (my reading).**
    75	1. **On this repo, speed is not the reason to use a cache.** Every route takes milliseconds (BM-9). The difference is
    76	   in **what the route prints**: full-text prints about a sixth of grep's characters before naming a right file
    77	   (BM-6). Whether that becomes a sixth of an agent's tokens is not measured. Where
    78	   the question names a symbol or file, the graph gets there in tens of characters (BM-1, BM-3); its BM-6 mean is over
    79	   those 20 questions only, so it is not a like-for-like ratio.
    80	2. **The vector index loses almost everywhere it was tried.** It never found an exact identifier (BM-1), and it found
    81	   4% of the tests that run a function (BM-7). Its only edge is "how do I run or configure X" (BM-5, n=6), which is
    82	   too small to act on. Nothing here contradicts the earlier rule that vectors come last and only as a hybrid.
    83	3. **No route answers a concept asked in other words in one shot** (BM-2). That still takes an agent searching and
    84	   reading. It is exactly the miss the vector trigger is meant to record.
    85	4. **No single store answers "which tests should I run".** The graph finds tests that reach the code indirectly
    86	   (BM-7), but is blind to tests that run it as a separate process (BM-8). Full-text search on the file name finds
    87	   the latter. An empty graph answer means *not seen*, never *no tests*. Even combined, the two routes
    88	   can miss affected tests: both missed C05 entirely, and the graph found only 0.33 and 0.8 of the runtime tests for
    89	   C01 and C02 (BM-7). That is why the full suite runs before committing.
    90	
    91	**Routing rule for development and testing (proposed).**
    92	
    93	| the question | use | then | evidence |
    94	|---|---|---|---|
    95	| Where is function or method X defined? | code graph | read that definition | BM-1 |
    96	| Where is a flag, constant or string defined? | full-text, or grep | read the hit | BM-1 (A05) |
    97	| What calls X? | code graph | read the callers | BM-3 (C08–C10: 33–46 characters) |
    98	| Which tests should I run after changing function X or file F? | code graph (tests reaching X or F) **plus** full-text on the file name (tests that run it as a separate process) | run that subset; run the full suite (31–33 s) before committing | BM-7, BM-8, BM-9 |
    99	| Why did we decide X? Where is X explained in the docs? | full-text (ties hybrid on hits; prints less in total) | read the section | BM-4 |
   100	| How do I run or configure X? | not settled: hybrid and vector scored 0.5 and full-text 0.33, on 6 questions | read the hit | BM-5 (n=6, too small to act on) |
   101	| A concept, in my own words | grep and read, as an agent search | record a miss if the answer is found another way | BM-2 |
   102	| Anything the graph answers with nothing | full-text, then grep | — | BM-8 |
   103	
   104	This table is for the **code and docs**. Memory entries are covered by the per-store sections below. The two
   105	differ because the corpus differs. Code and docs exist today and are already large enough that full-text and the
   106	graph save reading (BM-6). `project-memory/` does not exist yet.
   107	
   108	**What this does not change, and what it proposes.**
   109	- **Unchanged:** the rules for all three caches below (full rebuild, rebuild when stale, fall back when absent) and
   110	  R1–R8 apply as written.
   111	- **Conflict with "The answer first" and the vision:** for code and docs, the measurements say full-text and the graph
   112	  already save reading today. That contradicts "none of the three caches is needed today" and the vision's *who it is
   113	  for: an agent retrieving an entry*. Changing either is EJ's call. The proposed wording is in
   114	  `runs/20261012-caches-routing/vision-proposals.md`, and neither file has been edited.
   115	
   116	## Rules for all three caches
   117	
   118	These apply to whichever cache is built. They come from how the researched tools fail.
   119	
   120	- **Full rebuild, never patched.** A live, incrementally updated index drifted silently from a clean rebuild: 4.3% of
   121	  edges wrong, with every integrity check passing (`G-F5 drift`). Its makers decided drift cannot be measured cheaply
   122	  (`G-F5c`). A full rebuild of this repo's code graph takes 0.94 s (`G-F12`); an FTS rebuild of 10,000 synthetic
   123	  entries takes 0.53 s (`S-F13`).
   124	- **Rebuild before answering when stale.** Before each query, compare the newest source file with the index and
   125	  rebuild first if the source is newer. Build into a temporary file and rename it over the old one (`S-F9`, quoted:
   126	  *"A stale index is worse than no index."*).
   127	- **Fall back when absent.** If the cache file or SQLite's FTS5 is missing, the tool warns once and answers with grep
   128	  (`S-F9`); the same for a missing embedding model (my reading, extending `S-F9`). A mistyped database path silently
   129	  creates an empty database, which looks exactly like a stale one (`G-F5 drift`, failure mode 5), so the tool opens the
   130	  cache read-only and treats "no such file" as absent.
   131	- **Index only active entries**, the same set as `INDEX.md`, with `status` and `covers` stored beside each row
   132	  (`V-F22`, my reading). Otherwise superseded entries come back looking current.
   133	
   134	## SQLite full-text index
   135	
   136	### Five whys
   137	
   138	1. **Why a full-text index?** To find entries by their words, ranked, with a snippet, instead of grep's unranked list
   139	   (`S-F1`, quoted: *"grep returns unranked matches."*).
   140	2. **Why does ranking matter?** Every hit the agent opens costs context. In the one controlled study, BM25 used 5.8K
   141	   tokens per question against 226K for an agent using grep over files, on its smallest corpus: 1,144 documents,
   142	   1.7M tokens (`S-F4`).
   143	3. **Why not build it now?** That study puts the point where BM25 overtakes the grep agent at around 10 million tokens
   144	   of text, and the grep agent led at the smallest sizes (`S-F4`). At our entry size that is about 37,000 entries
   145	   (`S-F5`, my reading via its 4-characters-per-token assumption). `INDEX.md` already lists every active entry. In the
   146	   researcher's own test, at 10,000 synthetic entries a one-word grep took 0.038 s and a grep chain for a header
   147	   question 0.06 s; at 50,000 the header chain took 0.15 s, and the one-word timing was invalid (`S-F13`). No study
   148	   covers a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short entries (`S-F5`: not measured).
   149	4. **Why might it be needed later?** `INDEX.md` stops being enough once it can no longer list every active entry under
   150	   its size limit. The agent then loses the one-line scan of every entry, and ranked search is what replaces it (my
   151	   reading).
   152	5. **Why only as a cache?** The markdown is the source of truth, and the cache must be rebuildable and skippable (M6).
   153	
   154	Structured questions such as "every active decision covering path X" or "what supersedes Y" do not need SQLite. A
   155	short script reading the entry headers answers them in 0.1 s at 10,000 entries (`S-F13`). That measures time, not
   156	tokens; my reading is that the script and an SQL query return the same list of entries, so the agent reads the same
   157	amount either way (not measured).
   158	
   159	### 5W1H
   160	
   161	| | |
   162	|---|---|
   163	| **Who** | Built and rebuilt by a script with no model call. Read by any agent on demand. EJ decides to build it when the trigger fires. |
   164	| **What** | One FTS5 table with `kind`, `status` and `covers` as `UNINDEXED` columns (`S-F2`). One row per active entry file: the research leaves the row grain open (`S-F17`); my reading is that an entry of one or two sentences is already one section. The default `unicode61` tokenizer: in the researcher's own one-row test, a plausible change to keep paths whole made ordinary queries return zero rows silently, and ids such as `D-0012` had to be quoted (`S-F16`). |
   165	| **When** | Built when the trigger fires: `INDEX.md` can no longer list every active entry. Rebuilt before any query that finds it stale. Never read at session start. |
   166	| **Where** | `project-memory/.cache/index.sqlite`, gitignored. |
   167	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   168	| **How** | One stdlib `sqlite3` script, the smallest option in the SQLite node (§5, option B): rebuild to a temporary file and rename; compare modification times for staleness; fall back to `rg`. Nothing to install: SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5 works here (verified 2026-10-12). |
   169	
   170	## Vector index
   171	
   172	### Five whys
   173	
   174	1. **Why a vector index?** To find an entry by meaning when the question shares no words with it.
   175	2. **Why can't grep or full-text search do that?** They match words. But the evidence does not show vectors winning on
   176	   memory text. (They do win on some code tasks, `V-F5`, and on half the file-based pairs in `V-F3`.) On conversational
   177	   memory, grep returned inline beat vector search for every harness and model pair (`V-F3`). On a 500,000-document
   178	   corpus, BM25 beat vectors even on the questions built to favour them (`V-F4`). On decision-style records, a
   179	   structured graph and a vector memory tool were "largely equivalent" on relevance, and plain notes search did no
   180	   better on listing or supersession questions (s3-F34; `V-F1`).
   181	3. **Why not build it now?** No measurement exists on a corpus shaped like `entries/` (`V-F11`: not measured).
   182	   Anthropic's 2024 guidance is that under about 200,000 tokens a knowledge base can simply be put in the prompt
   183	   (`V-F8`), roughly 400–650 entries (`V-F12`, my reading). This design does not do that (it never loads the whole
   184	   tree), so this is only a bound: below it, retrieval quality is not what limits an agent (my reading). Vectors also
   185	   add failures of their own: negation (`V-F20`), exact ids and paths (`V-F21`), and a second rebuild step that can lag
   186	   the first (`V-F24`).
   187	4. **Why might it be needed later?** If agents keep missing entries that exist because their words differ from the
   188	   entry's. The trigger is a record of such misses: the entry existed, `INDEX.md`, grep and full-text search all missed
   189	   it, and it was found another way (`V-F13`, my reading). How many misses justify building it is **EJ's number**. No
   190	   source gives one.
   191	5. **Why hybrid only?** On memory-style text, the positive results found combine vectors with keyword search (`V-F6`,
   192	   `V-F7`; `V-F10` is unproven), and vectors alone were the weakest arm when results were returned inline (`V-F3`,
   193	   `V-F4`). So the vector index comes after the full-text index, never
   194	   instead of it.
   195	
   196	### 5W1H
   197	
   198	| | |
   199	|---|---|
   200	| **Who** | Built and rebuilt by a script with no model call except the embedding model itself. Read by any agent on demand, through the same command as full-text search. EJ decides to build it when the trigger fires. |
   201	| **What** | One vector per active entry file, with the entry's `status`, `covers`, content hash and embedding-model id stored beside it (`V-F24`: a changed model makes every vector useless). Results merged with the full-text results by reciprocal rank fusion (vector node §Q5, from qmd's README). |
   202	| **When** | After the full-text index exists, and after the miss record shows EJ's number. Rebuilt with the full-text index, in the same step, so the two cannot disagree (`V-F24`, my reading). |
   203	| **Where** | `project-memory/.cache/`, beside `index.sqlite`, gitignored. |
   204	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   205	| **How** | The smallest options in `V-F18`/`V-F19`: a static Model2Vec model with numpy (option A, no GPU) or `bge-small-en-v1.5` through FastEmbed (option B). Both are small next to the 16 GB GPU here. Embedding speed on this GPU is **not measured** (`V-F18`). No embedding runtime is installed today; numpy is (`V-F19`). |
   206	
   207	## Code graph
   208	
   209	### Five whys
   210	
   211	1. **Why a code graph?** To answer structural questions about the code: what an edit touches, and whether the code an
   212	   entry covers has changed.
   213	2. **Why would memory need that?** For the drift check (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` line 75: mark a decision or fact whose
   214	   code changed with no earlier change to the entry) and for "which entries cover the files I am editing".
   215	3. **Why is git enough for both?** `covers:` names **paths**, not symbols, and is already a hand-kept index from entry
   216	   to path (`G-F14`). Two `git log` calls compare the entry's last change with its paths' last change, in 0.00–0.01 s
   217	   on this repo (`G-F12`; code-graph node §6). Git is the record itself, so it cannot drift.
   218	4. **Why not add a graph for precision anyway?** An incrementally synced graph adds a silent failure that git does not
   219	   have (`G-F5 drift`, 4.3% of edges wrong). A full-rebuild graph such as the repo's own does not drift (`G-F18`), but
   220	   it covers Python only, so for other `covers:` paths it gives a silent partial answer (code-graph node §5, option B).
   221	   The precision it buys, a one-line comment flagging a whole file, can be had by parsing only the changed file with
   222	   Python's `ast` (code-graph node §5, option E). The deeper question, "is this entry still true", belongs to the
   223	   background subagent that compares entries with the code (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` lines 68–73), not to a model-free
   224	   script.
   225	5. **Why might it be needed later?** Any one of the three conditions in the code-graph node's §6, each a test:
   226	   - `covers:` starts naming functions or classes instead of files;
   227	   - the check must answer about files not on any `covers:` list ("what does this change affect");
   228	   - path-level flags raise too many false alarms. How many is **EJ's number**.
   229	
   230	### 5W1H
   231	
   232	| | |
   233	|---|---|
   234	| **Who** | The drift-check script, with no model call. EJ decides to build a graph if one of the three conditions holds. |
   235	| **What** | Today: no graph. Git on `covers:` paths, plus an `ast` parse of changed Python files if precision is needed. If a condition holds: the repo's own `code_graph/code_graph.py` (Python only, deterministic, full rebuild, no incremental mode, `G-F18`) before any outside tool. |
   236	| **When** | Today the check runs whenever `INDEX.md` is generated (my reading of `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` line 75). A graph, if ever built, is rebuilt in full each time it is used. |
   237	| **Where** | Nothing today. Later, `project-memory/.cache/code-graph.json`, gitignored. |
   238	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   239	| **How** | Two `git log` calls per entry: `git log -1 --format=%at -- <entry file>` and the same for each `covers:` path (code-graph node §6). External indexers (codegraph) are not adopted: their win is measured on source-code architecture questions, not on memory (`G-F7`, `G-F14`), and they bring the drift in `G-F5 drift`. |
   240	
   241	## What this changes elsewhere (proposed, not done)
   242	
   243	- `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` lists as undecided "how the script tells that the entry changed earlier than the code". The
   244	  research suggests an answer for EJ to decide: compare `git log` times of the entry file and its `covers:` paths.
   245	- `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` line 104 can keep `.cache/` as written. This document says when each part of it is built.
   246	
   247	## Not known
   248	
   249	- Every trigger number is EJ's: the `INDEX.md` size limit (already open in `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`), the number of
   250	  recorded misses for vectors, and the false-alarm rate for the code graph.
   251	- No measurement exists of any of the three on a project-memory corpus. All evidence is on other corpora: source code,
   252	  chat memory, enterprise documents.
   253	- The researchers' web reads went through extracts. `S-F*` quotes are not byte-checked (`sqlite-fts.claude.md`,
   254	  header). `V-F*` quotes were checked against saved copies.
   255	- The planted "tool that does not exist" check in the research was void: the names were readable in the run's
   256	  `state.md` (see that file's log).

    27	> and the requirements each have one copy.
    28	
    29	## The answer first
    30	
    31	None of the three caches is needed today. `project-memory/` does not exist yet, so nothing has been measured on it.
    32	My reading of the research is that at the sizes measured elsewhere, `INDEX.md`, grep and git answer the questions
    33	each cache would answer. Each cache below has a
    34	**trigger**: a condition that can be observed and that makes it worth building. None of them is built before its
    35	trigger fires.
    36	
    37	This follows the design's own rule: `.cache/` is optional, gitignored and rebuilt from the files, and its absence
    38	must not stop anyone reading memory (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`; recommendation M4, M6). It also follows the repo's own
    39	history of building graphs nobody queried (`docs/KNOWLEDGE_GRAPH_DESIGN.md` §1, quoted in `G-F21`).
    40	
    41	## When to use which store
    42	
    43	At query time the memory is answered by the files — `INDEX.md`, grep, git, or reading the entry — and a cache is
    44	never the answer to a question the files answer. The table below merges three engines asked independently, one store
    45	each: `claude` on the code graph, `codex` on the vector index, `opencode` on SQLite FTS5. The full 21-row table and
    46	the 30-row cost table, with every disagreement kept, are in `runs/20261012-caches-enhance/DECISION-RULES.md` — the
    47	run record. **This file is the live copy.**
    48	
    49	**The rule.** **SQLite FTS5** is for a free-text question whose words are likely in the entry text, once `INDEX.md`
    50	can no longer list every active entry under its size limit and ranked results with snippets save opened files
    51	(`S-F1`, `S-F4`). **The vector index** is for the same discovery when the question's wording differs from the
    52	entry's, once the documented miss trigger has fired (`V-F11`, `V-F13`, `M4`). **The code graph** is only for a
    53	multi-hop structural question about Python code, after its trigger, and only as a lower bound (`G-F8`, `G-F17`,
    54	`G-F18`, `M5`). Everything else is the files.
    55	
    56	| the question | store | if not, this | evidence |
    57	|---|---|---|---|
    58	| What do we already know about X? (free text, words likely in the entry) | **FTS5** — conditional on the size trigger | `INDEX.md`, grep, then read | S-F1, S-F4 |
    59	| Which prior entry matches when my words differ from its? (find by meaning) | **vector** — conditional on the miss trigger | `INDEX.md`, grep, then read | V-F11, V-F13, M4 |
    60	| What does changing F reach, several hops out, including files on no `covers:` list? | **code graph** — after its trigger; Python only; lower bound | grep imports and callers, then read | G-F8, G-F17, G-F18 |
    61	| Which tests reach function F, so I know what to run first? | **code graph** — conditional, lower bound | grep test names, then run the suite | G-F12, R5.3 |
    62	| An exact identifier, an error string, a known `file:line` | none | literal grep or filename lookup, then read | V-F7, V-F21, s3-F29 |
    63	| Every active entry covering path X — completeness | none — a ranked top-k cannot prove completeness | exhaustive scan of `INDEX.md` and entry headers | V-F21 |
    64	| Proving absence ("have we never recorded…") | none — no hit is not proof | exhaustive search, then read | V-F20 |
    65	| A structured header question (status, `supersedes`, `depends_on`) | none | `INDEX.md` scan or a script over frontmatter; follow links by grep | V-F22, M13 |
    66	| Why did we choose this, or why is this line like this? | none | the entry's **Why:** and its evidence; `git blame`, `git log -S` | s3-F4 |
    67	| Did the covered code change after the entry was verified? | none | `git log -1 --format=%at` on the entry, then on each `covers:` path | G-F14, G-F12, M15 |
    68	| Is this decision still true of the code? | none | read the entry and the code; the design gives this to the background subagent | SOURCE_OF_TRUTH:68–73 |
    69	| What should I load when resuming? | none | `INDEX.md` and the task's `state.md` | M17, M18, M19 |
    70	| A map of the whole repo | none | `PROJECT_OVERVIEW.md`, `INDEX.md` | M18, G-F18 |
    71	| A `covers:` path that is not Python | none | git and grep | G-F18 |
    72	| Is the graph I am about to query current? | none — do not check, rebuild whole | rebuild, or use git | G-F5, G-F18 |
    73	
    74	**Costs, and where there are none.** Measured on other corpora or on this machine, never on a real
    75	`project-memory/`: grep over 10,000 synthetic entries, 0.038 s for one word and 0.060 s for a structured chain
    76	(`S-F13`); grep plus opening every hit, about 226K tokens per question (`S-F4`, `S-F13` — opencode's figure, and D3
    77	records that the other two report no such measurement); an `INDEX.md` scan, under 0.01 s and minimal tokens
    78	(`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`); reading one entry, roughly 270 tokens by the 4-characters-per-token assumption (`S-F5`, not
    79	measured); a git last-change lookup at the tool's timing resolution (`G-F12`). **`GAP` for everything that matters
    80	most**: no FTS5-vs-grep measurement on short entries (`S-F5`), no vector measurement on a corpus shaped like
    81	`entries/` (`V-F11`), no graph-vs-grep head-to-head on this repo's own questions (`G-F10`, `M21`), and no wrapper
    82	exists to time a filtered graph query (`G-F18`). The last three are now measured on this repo's own code and
    83	docs. See **Measured here: development and testing** below.
    84	
    85	**Still unresolved.** Six disagreements the three engines did not settle, kept as positions and not averaged (D1–D6
    86	in the run record): whether FTS5 or the vector index comes first, or whether memory needs a store at all; whether the
    87	FTS/vector benefit has been measured at all; the true token cost of grep-plus-open; the cost of `INDEX.md`; whether
    88	`INDEX.md` carries `covers:` and status; and what counts as a *fired* trigger.
    89	
    90	**Why no SQLite is visible in this repo.** Three reasons, and none is a rejection. No `.db` is ever on disk — the
    91	patterns are gitignored and the indexes are rebuilt on demand. The SQLite this repo does have, `ancient_games/index.py`,
    92	indexes the **referee's journal**, not memory: it is the working precedent for this exact pattern (7 tables, derived,
    93	~8 ms per journal) but it answers a different question. And the memory cache is gated behind a trigger that has not
    94	fired, because `project-memory/` does not exist yet. The same is true of the code graph: `code_graph/code_graph.py`
    95	already exists, deterministic and full-rebuild, and is the option this document names if that trigger fires.
    96	
    97	A fourth reason belongs to the first draft. Its research asked only whether each cache is needed to search memory
    98	*entries*, so it measured nothing on the questions asked during development and testing. The next section measures
    99	those.
   100	
   101	## Measured here: development and testing
   102	
   103	EJ's aim is that development and testing get faster and cheaper in tokens. So this section measures all three stores
   104	against grep on **this repo**: 490 files (123 `.py`, 367 `.md`, 4.8 MB), with 40 questions an agent asks while
   105	developing and testing it. Run: `runs/20261012-caches-routing/` (results in `bench/results.md`, ids `BM-*` below).
   106	
   107	**Who wrote each check, and what it does not establish.**
   108	- The **questions and answers** were written by codex (`gpt-6.1-sol`), each checked by an `rg` command it ran. Five
   109	  Claude agents re-checked all 40: 37 were correct and 3 were incomplete. Those 3 were completed.
   110	- **"Which tests run this code"** is also scored against per-test runtime coverage (`pytest-cov`, no model). That
   111	  counts in-process Python only, so it misses tests that run the code as a separate process.
   112	- The **harness** was written by opencode. A Claude audit found 5 high-severity biases, and a Claude repair fixed
   113	  them; the repair was **not re-audited**. Rebuilding from scratch gave the same answers on all 180 question-route
   114	  pairs.
   115	- **Token cost** is measured as *characters printed up to the first line that names a right file*. That is a proxy,
   116	  not a token count, and a weak one:
   117	  - it stops at the right *file*, not the right answer;
   118	  - misses are left out, so each route's mean is over a different set of questions;
   119	  - each route prints a different format.
   120	
   121	  No agent was run end to end.
   122	- One repo, one question set, **n = 6 to 10 per category**. None of this is a project-memory corpus.
   123	
   124	**What each route returns, per category** (symbol hit in the top 5 / characters read to the first right answer):
   125	
   126	| | grep (`rg`) | SQLite full-text | vector | hybrid | code graph |
   127	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
   128	| **BM-1** exact identifier (8) | 0.75 / 920 | 0.63 / 356 | 0.0 / 837 | 0.5 / 91 | **0.75 / 43** |
   129	| **BM-2** concept in other words (8) | 0 / — | 0 / — | 0 (file 0.25) / 955 | 0 (file 0.13) / 1,427 | n/a |
   130	| **BM-3** structure and test impact (10) | 0.8 / 1,015 | **0.9 / 175** | 0.1 / 1,421 | 0.9 / 362 | 0.7 / **61** |
   131	| **BM-4** decision or reason in the docs (8) | 0.63 / 4,470 | **0.63 / 525** | 0.5 / 810 | 0.63 / 516 | n/a |
   132	| **BM-5** how to run or configure (6) | 0.17 / 6,875 | 0.33 / 1,146 | 0.5 / 569 | 0.5 / 1,120 | n/a |
   133	
   134	- **BM-6, all categories.** Characters printed up to the first right file (each mean over that route's hits only:
   135	  grep 26, full-text 25, vector 19, hybrid 26, graph 13): grep 2,688, full-text 451, hybrid 528, vector
   136	  923, graph 53 (graph over the 20 questions that name a symbol or file). Total output per answer: grep 4,304,
   137	  full-text 1,817.
   138	- **BM-7, test impact against runtime coverage** (C01–C04, n=4). The share of the tests that really run the function
   139	  each route found: graph 0.78; grep, full-text and hybrid 0.59; vector 0.04.
   140	- **BM-8, tests that run the code as a separate process** (C05–C07). The graph returns **nothing**, because no
   141	  call edge exists. Full-text search on the file name found 2 of 3; grep found 1 of 3 and printed up to 8.7K
   142	  characters.
   143	- **BM-9, speed.** Every route answers in at most 15 ms per question; the slowest was grep on E04, at 14.7 ms. Full rebuilds take 0.17 s for full-text (9.5 MB),
   144	  0.94 s for vector (10.2 MB) and 0.89 s for the graph (4.9 MB). The full test suite, 529 tests, takes 31–33 s (3
   145	  runs).
   146	
   147	**What the numbers say (my reading).**
   148	1. **On this repo, speed is not the reason to use a cache.** Every route takes milliseconds (BM-9). The difference is
   149	   in **what the route prints**: full-text prints about a sixth of grep's characters before naming a right file
   150	   (BM-6). Whether that becomes a sixth of an agent's tokens is not measured. Where
   151	   the question names a symbol or file, the graph gets there in tens of characters (BM-1, BM-3); its BM-6 mean is over
   152	   those 20 questions only, so it is not a like-for-like ratio.
   153	2. **The vector index loses almost everywhere it was tried.** It never found an exact identifier (BM-1), and it found
   154	   4% of the tests that run a function (BM-7). Its only edge is "how do I run or configure X" (BM-5, n=6), which is
   155	   too small to act on. Nothing here contradicts the earlier rule that vectors come last and only as a hybrid.
   156	3. **No route answers a concept asked in other words in one shot** (BM-2). That still takes an agent searching and
   157	   reading. It is exactly the miss the vector trigger is meant to record.
   158	4. **No single store answers "which tests should I run".** The graph finds tests that reach the code indirectly
   159	   (BM-7), but is blind to tests that run it as a separate process (BM-8). Full-text search on the file name finds
   160	   the latter. An empty graph answer means *not seen*, never *no tests*. Even combined, the two routes
   161	   can miss affected tests: both missed C05 entirely, and the graph found only 0.33 and 0.8 of the runtime tests for
   162	   C01 and C02 (BM-7). That is why the full suite runs before committing.
   163	
   164	**Routing rule for development and testing (proposed).**
   165	
   166	| the question | use | then | evidence |
   167	|---|---|---|---|
   168	| Where is function or method X defined? | code graph | read that definition | BM-1 |
   169	| Where is a flag, constant or string defined? | full-text, or grep | read the hit | BM-1 (A05) |
   170	| What calls X? | code graph | read the callers | BM-3 (C08–C10: 33–46 characters) |
   171	| Which tests should I run after changing function X or file F? | code graph (tests reaching X or F) **plus** full-text on the file name (tests that run it as a separate process) | run that subset; run the full suite (31–33 s) before committing | BM-7, BM-8, BM-9 |
   172	| Why did we decide X? Where is X explained in the docs? | full-text (ties hybrid on hits; prints less in total) | read the section | BM-4 |
   173	| How do I run or configure X? | not settled: hybrid and vector scored 0.5 and full-text 0.33, on 6 questions | read the hit | BM-5 (n=6, too small to act on) |
   174	| A concept, in my own words | grep and read, as an agent search | record a miss if the answer is found another way | BM-2 |
   175	| Anything the graph answers with nothing | full-text, then grep | — | BM-8 |
   176	
   177	This table is for the **code and docs**. The table above it is for **memory entries**, and is unchanged. The two
   178	differ because the corpus differs. Code and docs exist today and are already large enough that full-text and the
   179	graph save reading (BM-6). `project-memory/` does not exist yet.
   180	
   181	**What this does not change, and what it proposes.**
   182	- **Unchanged:** the rules for all three caches below (full rebuild, rebuild when stale, fall back when absent) and
   183	  R1–R8 apply as written.
   184	- **Conflict with "The answer first" and the vision:** for code and docs, the measurements say full-text and the graph
   185	  already save reading today. That contradicts "none of the three caches is needed today" and the vision's *who it is
   186	  for: an agent retrieving an entry*. Changing either is EJ's call. The proposed wording is in
   187	  `runs/20261012-caches-routing/vision-proposals.md`, and neither file has been edited.
   188	
   189	## Rules for all three caches
   190	
   191	These apply to whichever cache is built. They come from how the researched tools fail.
   192	
   193	- **Full rebuild, never patched.** A live, incrementally updated index drifted silently from a clean rebuild: 4.3% of
   194	  edges wrong, with every integrity check passing (`G-F5 drift`). Its makers decided drift cannot be measured cheaply
   195	  (`G-F5c`). A full rebuild of this repo's code graph takes 0.94 s (`G-F12`); an FTS rebuild of 10,000 synthetic
   196	  entries takes 0.53 s (`S-F13`).
   197	- **Rebuild before answering when stale.** Before each query, compare the newest source file with the index and
   198	  rebuild first if the source is newer. Build into a temporary file and rename it over the old one (`S-F9`, quoted:
   199	  *"A stale index is worse than no index."*).
   200	- **Fall back when absent.** If the cache file or SQLite's FTS5 is missing, the tool warns once and answers with grep
   201	  (`S-F9`); the same for a missing embedding model (my reading, extending `S-F9`). A mistyped database path silently
   202	  creates an empty database, which looks exactly like a stale one (`G-F5 drift`, failure mode 5), so the tool opens the
   203	  cache read-only and treats "no such file" as absent.
   204	- **Index only active entries**, the same set as `INDEX.md`, with `status` and `covers` stored beside each row
   205	  (`V-F22`, my reading). Otherwise superseded entries come back looking current.
   206	
   207	## SQLite full-text index
   208	
   209	### Five whys
   210	
   211	1. **Why a full-text index?** To find entries by their words, ranked, with a snippet, instead of grep's unranked list
   212	   (`S-F1`, quoted: *"grep returns unranked matches."*).
   213	2. **Why does ranking matter?** Every hit the agent opens costs context. In the one controlled study, BM25 used 5.8K
   214	   tokens per question against 226K for an agent using grep over files, on its smallest corpus: 1,144 documents,
   215	   1.7M tokens (`S-F4`).
   216	3. **Why not build it now?** That study puts the point where BM25 overtakes the grep agent at around 10 million tokens
   217	   of text, and the grep agent led at the smallest sizes (`S-F4`). At our entry size that is about 37,000 entries
   218	   (`S-F5`, my reading via its 4-characters-per-token assumption). `INDEX.md` already lists every active entry. In the
   219	   researcher's own test, at 10,000 synthetic entries a one-word grep took 0.038 s and a grep chain for a header
   220	   question 0.06 s; at 50,000 the header chain took 0.15 s, and the one-word timing was invalid (`S-F13`). No study
   221	   covers a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand short entries (`S-F5`: not measured).
   222	4. **Why might it be needed later?** `INDEX.md` stops being enough once it can no longer list every active entry under
   223	   its size limit. The agent then loses the one-line scan of every entry, and ranked search is what replaces it (my
   224	   reading).
   225	5. **Why only as a cache?** The markdown is the source of truth, and the cache must be rebuildable and skippable (M6).
   226	
   227	Structured questions such as "every active decision covering path X" or "what supersedes Y" do not need SQLite. A
   228	short script reading the entry headers answers them in 0.1 s at 10,000 entries (`S-F13`). That measures time, not
   229	tokens; my reading is that the script and an SQL query return the same list of entries, so the agent reads the same
   230	amount either way (not measured).
   231	
   232	### 5W1H
   233	
   234	| | |
   235	|---|---|
   236	| **Who** | Built and rebuilt by a script with no model call. Read by any agent on demand. EJ decides to build it when the trigger fires. |
   237	| **What** | One FTS5 table with `kind`, `status` and `covers` as `UNINDEXED` columns (`S-F2`). One row per active entry file: the research leaves the row grain open (`S-F17`); my reading is that an entry of one or two sentences is already one section. The default `unicode61` tokenizer: in the researcher's own one-row test, a plausible change to keep paths whole made ordinary queries return zero rows silently, and ids such as `D-0012` had to be quoted (`S-F16`). |
   238	| **When** | Built when the trigger fires: `INDEX.md` can no longer list every active entry. Rebuilt before any query that finds it stale. Never read at session start. |
   239	| **Where** | `project-memory/.cache/index.sqlite`, gitignored. |
   240	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   241	| **How** | One stdlib `sqlite3` script, the smallest option in the SQLite node (§5, option B): rebuild to a temporary file and rename; compare modification times for staleness; fall back to `rg`. Nothing to install: SQLite 3.45.1 with FTS5 works here (verified 2026-10-12). |
   242	
   243	## Vector index
   244	
   245	### Five whys
   246	
   247	1. **Why a vector index?** To find an entry by meaning when the question shares no words with it.
   248	2. **Why can't grep or full-text search do that?** They match words. But the evidence does not show vectors winning on
   249	   memory text. (They do win on some code tasks, `V-F5`, and on half the file-based pairs in `V-F3`.) On conversational
   250	   memory, grep returned inline beat vector search for every harness and model pair (`V-F3`). On a 500,000-document
   251	   corpus, BM25 beat vectors even on the questions built to favour them (`V-F4`). On decision-style records, a
   252	   structured graph and a vector memory tool were "largely equivalent" on relevance, and plain notes search did no
   253	   better on listing or supersession questions (s3-F34; `V-F1`).
   254	3. **Why not build it now?** No measurement exists on a corpus shaped like `entries/` (`V-F11`: not measured).
   255	   Anthropic's 2024 guidance is that under about 200,000 tokens a knowledge base can simply be put in the prompt
   256	   (`V-F8`), roughly 400–650 entries (`V-F12`, my reading). This design does not do that (it never loads the whole
   257	   tree), so this is only a bound: below it, retrieval quality is not what limits an agent (my reading). Vectors also
   258	   add failures of their own: negation (`V-F20`), exact ids and paths (`V-F21`), and a second rebuild step that can lag
   259	   the first (`V-F24`).
   260	4. **Why might it be needed later?** If agents keep missing entries that exist because their words differ from the
   261	   entry's. The trigger is a record of such misses: the entry existed, `INDEX.md`, grep and full-text search all missed
   262	   it, and it was found another way (`V-F13`, my reading). How many misses justify building it is a **measured comparison** (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` § How each threshold is
   263	found), not a number to guess. No
   264	   source gives one.
   265	5. **Why hybrid only?** On memory-style text, the positive results found combine vectors with keyword search (`V-F6`,
   266	   `V-F7`; `V-F10` is unproven), and vectors alone were the weakest arm when results were returned inline (`V-F3`,
   267	   `V-F4`). So the vector index comes after the full-text index, never
   268	   instead of it.
   269	
   270	### 5W1H
   271	
   272	| | |
   273	|---|---|
   274	| **Who** | Built and rebuilt by a script with no model call except the embedding model itself. Read by any agent on demand, through the same command as full-text search. EJ decides to build it when the trigger fires. |
   275	| **What** | One vector per active entry file, with the entry's `status`, `covers`, content hash and embedding-model id stored beside it (`V-F24`: a changed model makes every vector useless). Results merged with the full-text results by reciprocal rank fusion (vector node §Q5, from qmd's README). |
   276	| **When** | After the full-text index exists, and after the logged misses outweigh the cache's cost. Rebuilt with the full-text index, in the same step, so the two cannot disagree (`V-F24`, my reading). |
   277	| **Where** | `project-memory/.cache/`, beside `index.sqlite`, gitignored. |
   278	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   279	| **How** | The smallest options in `V-F18`/`V-F19`: a static Model2Vec model with numpy (option A, no GPU) or `bge-small-en-v1.5` through FastEmbed (option B). Both are small next to the 16 GB GPU here. Embedding speed on this GPU is **not measured** (`V-F18`). No embedding runtime is installed today; numpy is (`V-F19`). |
   280	
   281	## Code graph
   282	
   283	### Five whys
   284	
   285	1. **Why a code graph?** To answer structural questions about the code: what an edit touches, and whether the code an
   286	   entry covers has changed.
   287	2. **Why would memory need that?** For the drift check (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` line 75: mark a decision or fact whose
   288	   code changed with no earlier change to the entry) and for "which entries cover the files I am editing".
   289	3. **Why is git enough for both?** `covers:` names **paths**, not symbols, and is already a hand-kept index from entry
   290	   to path (`G-F14`). Two `git log` calls compare the entry's last change with its paths' last change, in 0.00–0.01 s
   291	   on this repo (`G-F12`; code-graph node §6). Git is the record itself, so it cannot drift.
   292	4. **Why not add a graph for precision anyway?** An incrementally synced graph adds a silent failure that git does not
   293	   have (`G-F5 drift`, 4.3% of edges wrong). A full-rebuild graph such as the repo's own does not drift (`G-F18`), but
   294	   it covers Python only, so for other `covers:` paths it gives a silent partial answer (code-graph node §5, option B).
   295	   The precision it buys, a one-line comment flagging a whole file, can be had by parsing only the changed file with
   296	   Python's `ast` (code-graph node §5, option E). The deeper question, "is this entry still true", belongs to the
   297	   background subagent that compares entries with the code (`SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` lines 68–73), not to a model-free
   298	   script.
   299	5. **Why might it be needed later?** Any one of the three conditions in the code-graph node's §6, each a test:
   300	   - `covers:` starts naming functions or classes instead of files;
   301	   - the check must answer about files not on any `covers:` list ("what does this change affect");
   302	   - path-level flags raise too many false alarms — the comparison is in `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` § How each threshold
   303	     is found. How many you will tolerate is the one policy value, and it is yours.
   304	
   305	### 5W1H
   306	
   307	| | |
   308	|---|---|
   309	| **Who** | The drift-check script, with no model call. EJ decides to build a graph if one of the three conditions holds. |
   310	| **What** | Today: no graph. Git on `covers:` paths, plus an `ast` parse of changed Python files if precision is needed. If a condition holds: the repo's own `code_graph/code_graph.py` (Python only, deterministic, full rebuild, no incremental mode, `G-F18`) before any outside tool. |
   311	| **When** | Today the check runs whenever `INDEX.md` is generated (my reading of `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` line 75). A graph, if ever built, is rebuilt in full each time it is used. |
   312	| **Where** | Nothing today. Later, `project-memory/.cache/code-graph.json`, gitignored. |
   313	| **Why** | The five whys above. |
   314	| **How** | Two `git log` calls per entry: `git log -1 --format=%at -- <entry file>` and the same for each `covers:` path (code-graph node §6). External indexers (codegraph) are not adopted: their win is measured on source-code architecture questions, not on memory (`G-F7`, `G-F14`), and they bring the drift in `G-F5 drift`. |
   315	
   316	## What this changes elsewhere (proposed, not done)
   317	
   318	- `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` lists as undecided "how the script tells that the entry changed earlier than the code". The
   319	  research suggests an answer for EJ to decide: compare `git log` times of the entry file and its `covers:` paths.
   320	- `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md` line 104 can keep `.cache/` as written. This document says when each part of it is built.
   321	
   322	## Not known
   323	
   324	- Every trigger number is EJ's: the `INDEX.md` size limit (already open in `SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md`), the number of
   325	  recorded misses for vectors, and the false-alarm rate for the code graph.
   326	- No measurement exists of any of the three on a project-memory corpus. All evidence is on other corpora: source code,
   327	  chat memory, enterprise documents.
   328	- The researchers' web reads went through extracts. `S-F*` quotes are not byte-checked (`sqlite-fts.claude.md`,
   329	  header). `V-F*` quotes were checked against saved copies.
   330	- The planted "tool that does not exist" check in the research was void: the names were readable in the run's
   331	  `state.md` (see that file's log).
   332	
   333	---
   334	
   335	## Core requirements
   336	
   337	Acceptance criteria are checkable or they are questions for EJ. Where the research gives no number, the criterion
   338	carries `OPEN:` rather than an invented value.
   339	
   340	### R1 — Derived and disposable
   341	The cache is computed from the markdown and never holds knowledge of its own (M4, M6).
   342	
   343	- **R1.1** Deleting the cache and rebuilding from the files restores every answer. *Example:* `rm -rf
   344	  project-memory/.cache/` → run the documented rebuild → the same query returns the same entries. (M6)
   345	- **R1.2** Memory is fully readable with no cache present. *Example:* with `.cache/` absent, session start and an
   346	  on-demand `grep` over `entries/` both work. (M6)
   347	- **R1.3** The cache directory is ignored by git and a rebuild leaves `git status --short` clean. (M6)
   348	
   349	### R2 — Full rebuild, never patched
   350	- **R2.1** The documented rebuild regenerates the whole index from the files; no incremental update path exists.
   351	  *Example:* the rebuild command has no "update" mode. (G-F5 drift)
   352	- **R2.2** The index records what it was built from, so a stale one is detectable. *Example:* the index stores the
   353	  newest source file's mtime or the commit, and the query tool compares before answering. (S-F9)
   354	
   355	### R3 — Stale never answers
   356	- **R3.1** Before answering, the tool compares the newest source file with the index and rebuilds first if the
   357	  source is newer, building into a temporary file and renaming over the old one. (S-F9)
   358	- **R3.2** A wrong path is never mistaken for an empty index: the cache is opened read-only and "no such file" is
   359	  treated as absent. *Example:* point the tool at a typo'd path → it warns and falls back to grep, it does not
   360	  create an empty database. (G-F5, failure mode 5)
   361	
   362	### R4 — Absence degrades, it does not fail
   363	- **R4.1** A missing cache file or a missing FTS5 warns once and answers with grep. (S-F9)
   364	- **R4.2** A missing embedding model does the same. *(my reading, extending S-F9.)*
   365	
   366	### R5 — Nothing is built before its trigger fires
   367	Each cache has an observable trigger. This is the requirement that keeps the whole layer honest. The query-time
   368	counterpart — which store to reach for once a trigger has fired — is the section **When to use which store** above.
   369	
   370	- **R5.1 Full-text** — built only when `INDEX.md` can no longer list every active entry under its size limit.
   371	  `OPEN: not measured — the limit is the comparison in SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md § How each threshold is found` (S-F5, S-F13: not measured for a corpus of a few hundred to a few thousand
   372	  short entries).
   373	- **R5.2 Vector** — built only after R5.1 has fired *and* the logged misses outweigh the cache's cost. A "miss" is
   374	  recorded when an entry existed and `INDEX.md`, grep and full-text search all missed it (V-F13).
   375	  `OPEN: not measured — the miss count is the comparison in SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md § How each threshold is found.` The vector index is hybrid only — appended to
   376	  full-text results by reciprocal rank fusion, never instead of them (V-F3, V-F4, V-F6, V-F7; V-F10 is unproven).
   377	- **R5.3 Code graph** — built only when one of three conditions holds: `covers:` starts naming functions or classes
   378	  rather than files; the check must answer about files on no `covers:` list; or path-level flags raise too many
   379	  false alarms. `OPEN: not measured — the false-alarm comparison is in SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md § How each threshold is found.` (code-graph §6, G-F14, G-F18)
   380	
   381	### R6 — Only active entries, with their status
   382	- **R6.1** The index holds the same set of entries as `INDEX.md`, each row carrying `status` and `covers`.
   383	  *Example:* a superseded entry never appears in a search result as if current. (V-F22)
   384	
   385	### R7 — An answer names its entry
   386	- **R7.1** Every result prints the entry file it came from, so a human can open the source before acting.
   387	  *Example:* search output names `entries/D-0012-*.md`. (M19)
   388	
   389	### R8 — The rebuild stays cheap
   390	- **R8.1** The documented rebuild completes without a model call, except the embedding step of R5.2.
   391	  *Example:* rebuild with no model credentials available; the FTS and code-graph paths still work. (M4, M6)
   392	- **R8.2** `OPEN: not measured — the budget is the comparison in SOURCE_OF_TRUTH.md § How each threshold is found.` Measured, for orientation only: 0.94 s (code graph,
   393	  this repo, G-F12) and 0.53 s (FTS, 10,000 synthetic entries, S-F13). Nothing is measured on a real
   394	  `project-memory/` corpus, because it does not exist yet.

    10	> `G-F12`). They show timings on this machine and nothing more.
